For
b1)
and
b2)
the reasoning is similar.
It follows that to get the uniqueness we need to be stricter.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
S=X(S)\sqcup Z(S)
be admissible for
v
.
Then
S
is
full admissible
if:

1.

for every
\gamma\in X(S)
long and
\alpha\in S
short such that
\suc(\alpha)\in\Phi^{+}(v)
,
\suc(\alpha)>\gamma
and
\alpha\ngtr\gamma
we have
\suc(\alpha)\in Z(S)
;

2.

for every
\gamma\in Z(S)
and
\alpha\in S
short such that
\suc(\alpha)\in\Phi^{+}(v)
,
\suc(\alpha)>\gamma
and
\alpha\ngtr\pad(\gamma)
we have
\suc(\alpha)\in Z(S)
.

It is easy to see that
a1)
and
b1)
verify Definition
6.3
while
a2)
and
b2)
don’t.
Actually, we can obtain
a1)
from
a2)
and
b1)
from
b2)
through the following process.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
S=X(S)\sqcup Z(S)
be admissible for
v
.
Denote

\displaystyle A(S)

\displaystyle=\left\{\beta\in\Phi^{+}(v)\setminus S\mid\exists\alpha\in S_{s},\beta=\suc(\alpha)\text{ and }\exists\gamma\in X(S)\text{ long},\beta>\gamma\text{ and }\alpha\ngtr\gamma\right\}

\displaystyle B(S)

\displaystyle=\left\{\beta\in\Phi^{+}(v)\setminus S\mid\exists\alpha\in S_{s},\beta=\suc(\alpha)\text{ and }\exists\gamma\in Z(S),\beta>\gamma\text{ and }\alpha\ngtr\pad(\gamma)\right\}

Then the set

\overline{S}=S\cup A(S)\cup B(S)

is called the
full admissible completion
of
S
.

Note that
S
is full admissible if and only if
A(S)=B(S)=\varnothing
.

We want to show that the full admissible completion of an admissible set
S
is indeed full admissible.
Note that
A(S),B(S)\subseteq\suc(S_{s})
, hence the roots in
A(S)
and
B(S)
are not orthogonal to exactly one root in
S
.
In particular,
\overline{S}
is admissible with partition
X(\overline{S})=X(S)
and
Z(\overline{S})=Z(S)\cup A(S)\cup B(S)
.
We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose we have
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\Psi
such that
\suc(\alpha)>\suc(\beta)>\suc(\gamma)
and
\alpha\ngtr\beta,\beta\ngtr\gamma
.
Then
\alpha\ngtr\gamma
.

Proof.

Note that it must be
\alpha,\beta
short.
Then we can write
\alpha=e_{i}+e_{j},\suc(\alpha)=2e_{i},\beta=e_{h}+e_{k},\suc(\beta)=2e_{h}
and the hypothesis imply
i<h<k<j
.

Now suppose
\gamma=e_{r}+e_{t}
short and
\suc(\gamma)=2e_{r}
with
r<t
.
Then
\suc(\gamma)<\suc(\beta)
implies
r>h
and
\beta\ngtr\gamma
implies
t<k
.
Hence,
i<h<r<t<k<j
.
If
\gamma=\suc(\gamma)=2e_{r}
a similar reasoning gives
i<h<r<k<j
.
In both cases the claim is proved.
∎

Theorem 6.6
.

Suppose that
S=X(S)\sqcup Z(S)
is admissible for
v
.
Then
\overline{S}
is full admissible for
v
.

Proof.

It is clear by the definition that
v(\overline{S})<0
, so we want to show
A(\overline{S})=B(\overline{S})=\varnothing
.

Suppose that we have
\gamma\in X(\overline{S})=X(S)
long and
\alpha\in\overline{S}
short with
\suc(\alpha)\in\Phi^{+}(v)
,
\suc(\alpha)>\gamma
,
\alpha\nless\gamma
and
\suc(\alpha)\notin Z(\overline{S})
.
Then
\alpha\in S
because to obtain
\overline{S}
we only added long roots.
Hence,
\suc(\alpha)\in A(S)\subseteq Z(\overline{S})
and we have a contradiction.

On the other hand, suppose we have
\gamma\in Z(\overline{S})=Z(S)\cup A(S)\cup B(S)
long and
\alpha\in\overline{S}
short with
\suc(\alpha)\in\Phi^{+}(v)
,
\suc(\alpha)>\gamma
,
\pad(\gamma)\nless\alpha
and
\suc(\alpha)\notin Z(\overline{S})
.
As before,
\alpha\in S
, hence we can have
\suc(\alpha)\notin Z(\overline{S})
only if
\gamma\notin Z(S)
.
But Lemma
6.5
shows us that in this hypothesis
\gamma\in A(S)
and
\gamma\in B(S)
both imply
\suc(\alpha)\in A(S)\cup B(S)\subseteq Z(\overline{S})
and that’s a contradiction.
∎

We have an easy corollary.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
S
be admissible.
Then
\overline{S}=S
if and only if
S
is full admissible.
In particular,
\overline{\overline{S}}=\overline{S}
for every
S
admissible.

Proof.

We already saw that
A(S)\cup B(S)=\varnothing
if and only if
S
is full admissible.
Then, the fact that
\overline{\overline{S}}=\overline{S}
is a consequence of Theorem
6.6
.
∎

Now, return to our pairs of examples
a1)
,
a2)
and
b1)
,
b2)
.
In both examples we are adding a single root denoted with
Z_{1}
.
However, the completion from
a2)
to
a1)
gives
A=\left\{Z_{1}\right\}
and
B=\varnothing
, while the completion from
b2)
to
b1)
gives
A=\varnothing
and
B=\left\{Z_{1}\right\}
.
This two examples completely model what may happen in general.
Specifically, the way we showed that the pairs of elements
a1)
,
a2)
and
b1)
,
b2)
are in the same
B_{v}
-orbit can be easily extended to generic elements of a
B_{v}
-orbit in
\mathfrak{p}^{u}
to obtain the following result.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
S
be admissible for
v
and
\overline{S}
its full admissible completion.
Then
B_{v}e_{S}=B_{v}e_{\overline{S}}
.

Now, if
S=X\sqcup Z
is admissible we will say that a root
\beta\in Z
is
essential
if
\beta\notin\overline{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}
.
This is equivalent by Corollary
6.7
to asking that
\overline{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}\neq\overline{S}
.
From what we said above we see that if
\beta
is not essential then
S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}
is still admissible and the elements
e_{S}
and
e_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}
are in the same orbit.

Moreover, note that if
\beta
is essential in
S
and
\beta<\alpha\in\Psi
, then
\beta
is essential also in
S\cap\left\{\gamma\in\Psi\mid\gamma<\alpha\right\}
.

Now suppose
S
admissible.
The following easy lemma will be useful later.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
S
be admissible for
v
.
Then
S
is linearly independent in
\Phi\otimes\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

For every
\beta_{i}\in Z(S)
there is
\gamma_{i}\in\Phi^{+}_{P}
such that
\pad(\beta_{i})+\gamma_{i}=\beta_{i}
.
Then
S
is linearly independent if and only if
S^{\prime}=\left(S\setminus\bigcup\left\{\beta_{i}\right\}\right)\cup\bigcup\left\{\gamma_{i}\right\}
is linearly independent.
But this is clear because the elements of
S^{\prime}
are all pairwise orthogonal.
∎

The following lemma is the key to prove that the full admissible pairs parametrize the orbits.
It basically gives us an algorithm to obtain an admissible representative of an orbit.
Then, we saw above that we can complete it to a full admissible representative for the same orbit.
Note that if
R\subseteq\Phi
and
\alpha\in\Phi
we will write
R>\alpha
meaning that
\beta>\alpha
for every
\beta\in R
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose
\beta\in\Phi^{+}(v)
and
S
admissible such that for every
\alpha\in S
,
\beta\nless\alpha
.
Moreover, suppose that
S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
is not admissible .
Then, for every
t\in\mathbb{K}
there is
u\in B_{L}
such that

\supp(u\left(e_{S}+te_{\beta}\right)-e_{S})>\beta

Proof.

Note that if
\gamma\in\supp(x)
is minimal, then
\gamma\in\supp(ux)
for every
u\in B_{L}
.
It follows that the thesis is equivalent to proving the existence of
u\in B_{L}
such that

\supp(ue_{S}-e_{S}-te_{\beta})>\beta

to see this, simply apply
u^{-1}\in B_{L}
.

Now put
X=X(S)
,
Z=Z(S)
and
S^{\prime}=S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
.
It must be
X\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
not orthogonal or
S^{\prime}
would be admissible with partitions
X(S^{\prime})=X\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
and
Z(S^{\prime})=Z
.
Similarly
\beta
must be short or
X(S^{\prime})=X
and
Z(S^{\prime})=Z\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
would be an admissible partition for
S^{\prime}
.
Then there exists
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{P}
and
\alpha\in X
such that
\beta=\alpha+\gamma
.
Note that
\alpha^{\prime}+\gamma\notin\Phi
for every
\alpha^{\prime}\in X\setminus\left\{\alpha\right\}
.

If
\alpha\notin\pad(Z)
, then
u_{\gamma}(s).e_{S}=e_{S}+ase_{\beta}+bs^{2}e_{\beta+\gamma}
for some constant
a\in\mathbb{K}^{*}
and
b\in\mathbb{K}
.
It follows that there is
s_{0}\in\mathbb{K}^{*}
for which

\supp(u_{\gamma}(s_{0}).e_{S}-\left(e_{S}+te_{\beta}\right))>\beta

If there is
\delta\in Z
with
\alpha=\pad(\delta)
, we have
u_{\gamma}(s).e_{\delta}=e_{\delta}+cse_{\delta+\gamma}+ds^{2}e_{\delta+2\gamma}
for some
c,d\in\mathbb{K}
(not necessarily different from zero).
But
\beta=\alpha+\gamma<\delta+\gamma<\delta+2\gamma
, so we can still find
s_{0}\in\mathbb{K}^{*}
such that
u_{\gamma}(s_{0}).e_{S}
has the property we are looking for.
∎

Theorem 6.11
.

Every orbit
\mathcal{O}
contains an element of the form
e_{S}
where
S
is admissible.

Proof.

Take an element
x\in\mathcal{O}
.
We can assume without loss of generality that
S=\supp(x)\subseteq\Phi^{+}(v)
.
If
S
is admissible, by Lemma
6.9

S
is independent, hence there is an element
t
in the torus
T
such that
t.x=e_{S}
and the claim is proved.
Suppose
S
not admissible and define an ascending chain in
S

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}S_{1}=\min(S)\\
S_{i+1}=S_{i}\cup\min(S\setminus S_{i})\par\end{array}\right.

Put
x=\sum_{\alpha\in S}a_{\alpha}e_{\alpha}
.

Given that the elements of
S_{1}
are pairwise incomparable,
S_{1}
must be orthogonal.
Hence, there must be an
i_{0}\geq 1
such that
S_{i}
is admissible for every
i\leq i_{0}
but
S_{i_{0}+1}
is not.
Note that at most one long root can be in
\left(S_{i_{0}+1}\setminus S_{i_{0}}\right)
because the long roots are always comparable.

Denote
\mathcal{S}_{i_{0}+1}=\left\{\beta\in\left(S_{i_{0}+1}\setminus S_{i_{0}}\right)\mid\left(S_{i_{0}}\cup\left\{\beta\right\}\right)\text{ is admissible}\right\}
.
It is easy to see that
S_{i_{0}}\cup\mathcal{S}
is also admissible.
For, there is at most a long root
\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{i_{0}+1}
and all the other roots must be orthogonal to
S_{i_{0}}
.
Given that all the roots in
\mathcal{S}_{i_{0}+1}
are pairwise orthogonal it is clear that
S^{\prime}=S_{i_{0}}\cup\left\{\mathcal{S}_{i_{0}+1}\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}\right)
is admissible.
Now,
\beta
is a long root which is not smaller than any root in
S^{\prime}
, hence
S^{\prime}\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
is still admissible.
Given that
S^{\prime}
is admissible, we can suppose by Lemma
6.9
that
a_{\alpha}=1
for every
\alpha\in S^{\prime}
.

Now if
\beta\in\left(S_{i_{0}+1}\setminus S^{\prime}\right)
we know by Lemma
6.10
that there is
u\in B_{L}
such that

\supp\left(u.(e_{S^{\prime}}+a_{\beta}e_{\beta})-e_{S^{\prime}}\right)>\beta

Then
u.x
is such that
S_{j}(u.x)=S_{j}(x)
for every
j\leq i_{0}
and
S_{i_{0}+1}(u.x)=S_{i_{0}+1}(x)\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}
.
By induction we obtain the thesis.
∎

We have an easy corollary that comes directly from the proof of Theorem
6.11
and will be useful later.

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a
B_{v}
-orbit and
x\in\mathcal{O}
.
Suppose
S\subseteq\supp(x)
admissible such that for every
\alpha\in\left(\supp(x)\setminus S\right)
and for every
\gamma\in S
we have
\alpha\nleq\gamma
.
Then, if
\beta\in\min\left(\supp(x)\setminus S\right)
and
S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
is admissible there is
T\supseteq S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
admissible such that
\mathcal{O}=B_{v}e_{T}
.

Proof.

Put
R=\supp(x)
.
Then, with the notation of Theorem
6.11
there is a minimum
j
such that
S\subseteq R_{j}
.

If
S=R_{J}
, then
\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{j+1}
, hence the proof of Theorem
6.11
gives us an admissible set
T\supseteq S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
such that
\mathcal{O}=B_{v}e_{T}
.

If instead
S\subsetneq R_{j}
, given that there is no element in
R\setminus S
smaller than a element in
S
and
S
is admissible it must be
R_{j-1}\subseteq S\subseteq R_{j-1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{j}
.
Moreover, given that
S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
is also admissible and
\beta
is minimal it must be
S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}\subseteq R_{j-1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{j}
.
As before, the proof of Theorem
6.11
shows us that there is
T\supseteq R_{j-1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{j}
such that
\mathcal{O}=B_{v}e_{T}
.
∎

We saw that there is a
P
-equivariant map

\exp\colon\mathfrak{p^{u}}\longrightarrow P^{\mathfrak{u}}

Recall that
B_{v}=B_{L}\ltimes U_{v}
, hence if
x\neq y\in\mathcal{O}
we have
Bv\exp(x)v^{-1}B=Bv\exp(y)v^{-1}B
and that if
S=\supp(x)
is orthogonal, then
Bv\exp(x)v^{-1}B=Bv\sigma_{S}v^{-1}B
where
\sigma_{S}=\prod_{\alpha\in S}s_{\alpha}
is the involution related to
S
.

Consider
S=X\cup Z
admissible and recall that for every element
\alpha\in S
there is at most one other
\beta\in S
such that
(\alpha,\beta)\neq 0
.
In this case, we can suppose
\alpha<\beta
and we have
\alpha\in X
,
\beta\in Z
.
Denote
\gamma=\beta-\alpha
.
We have
s_{\alpha}(\alpha+\gamma)=\gamma-\alpha\in-\Phi^{+}(v)
and
s_{\alpha+\gamma}(\alpha)=-\gamma\in\Phi^{-}_{P}
.
Note that this follows only from the relative length of the roots involved, so it is true even in type
\bf{B}
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Fix an orbit
\mathcal{O}=B_{v}e_{S}
where
S=X\cup Z
is admissible for
v
.
Then,

Bv\exp(e_{S})v^{-1}B=Bv\sigma_{X}v^{-1}B=B\sigma_{v\left(X\right)}B

Proof.

We have
Bv\exp(e_{S})v^{-1}B=Bv\exp(e_{Z})\exp(e_{X})v^{-1}B
.
By hypothesis,
v(S)<0
so both
v(-X)
and
v(-Z)
are in
B
.
Recall that we can chose
x_{\alpha}
such that

\exp(-x_{-\alpha})\exp(x_{\alpha})\exp(-x_{-\alpha})=s_{\alpha}

where
s_{\alpha}
is a representative in the normalizer
N_{G}(T)
for the reflection relative to
\alpha
.
The sets
X
and
Z
are orthogonal (relatively to themselves), so we can write:

Bv\exp(e_{Z})\exp(e_{X})v^{-1}B=Bvs_{Z}\exp(e_{-Z})\exp(e_{-X})s_{X}v^{-1}B

Note that the terms
\exp(e_{-Z})
and
\exp(e_{-X})
commute so

\displaystyle Bvs_{Z}\exp(e_{-Z})\exp(e_{-X})s_{X}v^{-1}B

\displaystyle=Bvs_{Z}\exp(e_{-X})\exp(e_{-Z})s_{X}v^{-1}B

\displaystyle=Bv\exp(e_{-s_{Z}(X)})s_{Z}s_{X}\exp(e_{-s_{X}(Z)})v^{-1}B

Note now that
-vs_{Z}(X)>0
because an element of
X
is either fixed by
s_{Z}
or mapped in
\Phi^{-}_{P}
by the discussion above and
-vs_{X}(Z)<0
because every element in
Z
is mapped in
-\Phi^{+}(v)
.
We have

\displaystyle Bv\exp(e_{-s_{Z}(X)})s_{Z}s_{X}\exp(e_{-s_{X}(Z)})v^{-1}B

\displaystyle=Bvs_{Z}s_{X}\exp(e_{s_{X}(Z)})s_{s_{X}(Z)}v^{-1}B

\displaystyle=Bvs_{Z}\exp(e_{Z})s_{X}s_{s_{X}(Z)}v^{-1}B

\displaystyle=Bv\exp(e_{-Z})s_{Z}s_{X}s_{s_{X}(Z)}v^{-1}B=

\displaystyle=Bvs_{Z}s_{X}s_{s_{X}(Z)}v^{-1}B

because
-v(Z)>0
.
The claim follows from
s_{Z}s_{X}s_{s_{X}(Z)}=s_{Z}s_{X}(s_{X}s_{Z}s_{X})=s_{X}
.
∎

It follows from Corollary
3.8
that if
B_{v}e_{S}=B_{v}e_{T}
with
S
and
T
admissible then
X(S)=X(T)
.
To prove the last part of the classification we will need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 6.14
.

Fix
v\in W^{P}
and let
\left(S,2e_{i},e_{i}+e_{j},b\right)
be a quadruple with
S\subseteq\Phi^{+}(v)
admissible,
e_{i}+e_{j},2e_{i}\in\Phi^{+}(v)\setminus S
and
b\in B_{v}
such that:

1.

S<2e_{i}
;

2.

S\cup\left\{2e_{i},e_{i}+e_{j}\right\}
is admissible and
2e_{i}
is essential;

3.

S\cup\left\{2e_{i}\right\}\subseteq\supp(b.e_{S})
.

Then there is
\alpha\in\supp(b.e_{S})\setminus S
such that
\alpha<2e_{i}
and
\alpha\ngeq e_{i}+e_{j}
.

Proof.

Write
\beta=2e_{i}
,
\pad(\beta)=e_{i}+e_{j}
and
T=S\cup\left\{e_{i}+e_{j}\right\}
.

Consider the set

K=\left\{b\in B_{v}\mid S\cup\left\{2e_{i}\right\}\subseteq\supp(b.e_{S})\right\}

which by assumption is non-empty.
Given that
T\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
is admissible,
\beta
must be orthogonal to
S
, so
S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
must be admissible and
X(S)\neq X(S\cup\left\{\beta\right\})
.
Hence, by Corollary
6.12
and Lemma
6.13
for every
b\in K
there must be a minimal root
\alpha_{b}\in\supp(be_{S})
such that
S\bigcup\left\{\alpha_{b}\right\}
is not admissible and
\alpha_{b}<\beta
.

Then the claim is equivalent to saying that

K^{\prime}=\left\{b\in K\mid\alpha_{b}\geq e_{i}+e_{j}\right\}=\varnothing

Suppose by contradiction that
K^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
and fix
b_{0}\in K^{\prime}
such that
\alpha_{b_{0}}
is maximal among the
\alpha_{b}
for
b\in K^{\prime}
.
Finally, denote for simplicity
\alpha=\alpha_{b_{0}}
.

Note that
\alpha\neq e_{i}+e_{j}
because
S\cup\left\{e_{i}+e_{j}\right\}
is admissible.
Then
\alpha=e_{i}+e_{k}
with
j>k>i
.
The situation can be represented with the following diagram.

\vphantom{1}\smash{\pad(\beta)}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\alpha}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\beta}

where the elements in
S
are not drawn, but we know they can’t be on the upper row because they must be orthogonal to
\pad(\beta)
.
There must be
\tau\in X(S)
that is not orthogonal to
\alpha
.
More precisely, there is
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{P}
such that
\alpha=\tau+\gamma
.
We will sign with a
\star
the possible positions of
\tau

\vphantom{1}\smash{\pad(\beta)}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\alpha}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\beta}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\star}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\star}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\star}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\star}

We claim that the one-parameter subgroup
u_{\gamma}(t)
acts as the identity on
e_{S\setminus\left\{\tau\right\}}
.
This is clear if
\suc(\tau)\notin Z(S)
, so suppose
\suc(\tau)\in Z(S)
.
If we write
\suc(\tau)=2e_{r}
and
\tau=e_{r}+e_{s}
with
r<s
, then we know that
r>i
.
Moreover, given that
2e_{i}
is essential, it must be
\tau<e_{i}+e_{j}
, hence
j<s
.
But now
\gamma=e_{r}+e_{s}
and
\alpha=e_{i}+e_{k}
are not orthogonal by hypothesis, and the only way to obtain this is if
r=k
(because
s>j>k>i
).
Then
\gamma=e_{i}-e_{s}
and is again the identity on
e_{2e_{k}}
.
Schematically, this means that if
\suc(\tau)\in Z(S)
then we cannot be in the situation of Diagram
a)
, hence we must be in the situation of Diagram
b)

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -66.42 M 0 -66.42 L 0 -44.28 L 38.74 -44.28 L 38.74 -66.42 Z M 38.74 -44.28}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 38.74 -66.42 M 38.74 -66.42 L 38.74 -44.28 L 77.49 -44.28 L 77.49 -66.42 Z M 77.49 -44.28}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -88.56 M 0 -88.56 L 0 -66.42 L 38.74 -66.42 L 38.74 -88.56 Z M 38.74 -66.42}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

because
\beta
is not essential in Diagram
a)
.

Note that given how we defined
\alpha
, every root in
\supp(b_{0}.e_{S})
that is smaller than
\beta
is either greater than
\alpha
,
\alpha
itself or in
S
, so
u_{\gamma}(t)
must act as the identity on every such root different from
\tau
.
Hence, there is
t\in\mathbb{K}^{*}
such that
2e_{i}\in\supp(u_{\gamma}(t)b.e_{S})
and
S\subseteq\supp(u_{\gamma}(t)b_{0}.e_{S})
, so
b^{\prime}=u_{\gamma}(t)b_{0}\in K
.
But
\alpha_{b^{\prime}}>\alpha
and that’s absurd because
\alpha=\alpha_{b_{0}}
was maximal.
∎

Theorem 6.15
.

Let
S,T\subseteq\Phi^{+}(v)
be full admissible.
Then

B_{v}e_{S}=B_{v}e_{T}\text{ if and only if }S=T

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
T\neq S
and take
\beta
minimal in
\left(T\setminus S\right)\cup\left(S\setminus T\right)
.
Without loss of generality, we can suppose
\beta\in T\setminus S
.

Consider
b\in B_{v}
such that
be_{S}=e_{T}
.
By Lemma
6.13
and Corollary
3.8
it must be
X(S)=X(T)
, hence actually
\beta\in Z(T)\setminus Z(S)
.
Then if
M_{\beta}=\left\{\alpha\in\Psi\mid\alpha<\beta\right\}
it must be
S\cap M_{\beta}=T\cap M_{\beta}\doteqdot M
.
Note that both
M
and
M\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
are admissible.

Now,
\beta
is long, so it must be of the form
\beta=2e_{i}
and if
\pad(\beta)=e_{i}+e_{j}\in X(T)
, then
e_{i}+e_{j}\in X(S)
also.
Denote
M^{\prime}=M\setminus\left\{e_{i}+e_{j}\right\}
.
We want to apply Lemma
6.14
to the quadruple
\left(M^{\prime},\beta,\pad(\beta),b\right)
to obtain a contradiction.
We know by hypothesis that
2e_{i}\in\supp(be_{M})
and
2e_{i}\notin\supp(be_{e_{i}+e_{j}})
by Lemma
6.1
.
Hence,
2e_{i}\in\supp(be_{M^{\prime}})
.
Moreover,
\beta
must be essential for
M\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
because if
\beta\in\overline{M}
, then also
\beta\in\overline{S}=S
.

It follows by Lemma
6.14
that there is
\alpha\in\supp(be_{M^{\prime}})\setminus M^{\prime}
such that
\alpha<\beta
and
\alpha\ngeq e_{i}+e_{j}
.
But
\alpha\ngeq e_{i}+e_{j}
implies
\alpha\notin\supp(be_{e_{i}+e_{j}})
, hence
\alpha\in\supp(be_{M})\setminus M
.
On the other hand, it is clear that if
\alpha\in\supp(be_{\gamma})
, then
\gamma<\alpha<\beta
, hence
\alpha
can’t be in the support of
be_{\gamma}
for every
\gamma\nless\beta
.
This means that we must have
\alpha\in\supp(be_{S})=T
and that’s a contradiction.
∎

7
The dimension of the
B_{v}
-orbits

We want to calculate the dimension of a
B_{v}
-orbit in
\mathfrak{p}_{u}
or equivalently
B
-orbits in
G/L
.

In the next couple of paragraphs, we drop the hypothesis that the field
\mathbb{K}
has characteristic 2.

Definition 7.1
.

For every
v\in W^{P}
let
\mathcal{S}^{v}=\left\{S^{v}_{i}\right\}_{i}
be a family of subsets
S^{v}_{i}\subseteq\Phi^{+}(v)
that parametrizes the
B_{v}
-orbits in
\mathfrak{p}^{u}
through the map
S^{v}_{i}\mapsto B_{v}e_{S^{v}_{i}}
.
Let
\mathcal{S}
be the family of these parametrizations.
We know that
\mathcal{S}
parametrizes the
B
-orbits in
G/L
.

We will say that
\mathcal{S}
is a
good
parametrization if:

1.

for every
u<v
the map

\displaystyle S^{v}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow S^{u}

\displaystyle S_{i}^{v}

\displaystyle\longmapsto S_{i}^{v}\cap\Phi^{+}(u)

is well-defined;

2.

\beta
maximal in
\Phi^{+}(v)
implies
S^{v}\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
independent for every
S^{v}\in\mathcal{S}^{v}
;

3.

B_{v}e_{S^{v}\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}=B_{v}e_{S^{v}}
implies that there is
b\in B_{L}
such that

\supp(be_{S^{v}}-e_{{S^{v}}\cup\left\{\beta\right\}})>\beta

Lemma 7.2
.

The following are good parametrizations:

1.

the parametrization for the type
B
-case given in
5.1
;

2.

the parametrization for the type
C
-case given in
1.1
;

3.

the parametrization for the non-characteristic-
2
cases given in
[
4
]

We can now prove the dimension formula.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a good parametrization and, with the notation above, fix
v\in W^{P}
and
S\in\mathcal{S}^{v}
.
Then, the dimension of
Bvx_{S}
is

\dim Bvx_{S}=\#\Psi+\#Y(v,S)

where
Y(v,S)=\left\{\beta\in\Phi^{+}(v)\mid\exists b\in B_{L}\text{ such that }\supp(be_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}-e_{S})>\beta\right\}
.

Proof.

We will show the claim by induction on
l(v)
.
If
l(v)=0
, then
Y=\varnothing
and
Bvx_{S}=BL/L\cong B/B_{L}\cong\mathfrak{p}_{u}
so the formula holds.

Now suppose
l(v)>0
and
\alpha\in\Delta
such that
s_{\alpha}v<v
.
Denote
\beta=v^{-1}(-\alpha)
.
Then
P_{\alpha}=Bs_{\alpha}\sqcup BU_{-\alpha}
, so

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}vx_{S}

\displaystyle=Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}\cup BU_{-\alpha}vx_{S}

\displaystyle=Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}\cup\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{K}}Bvu_{\beta}(t)x_{S}

\displaystyle=Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}\sqcup Bvx_{S}\cup\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{K}^{*}}Bvu_{\beta}(t)x_{S}

\displaystyle=Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}\sqcup Bvx_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}\sqcup Bvx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}

where we used the fact that
\beta\notin\Phi^{+}(s_{\alpha}v)
and that
S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
is independent.

The
B
-orbit
Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}
can’t be the open one because if
\overline{Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}}\supseteq Bvx_{S}
then it follows
\overline{Bs_{\alpha}vP}\supseteq BvP
and that’s impossible because
s_{\alpha}v<v
.

We show at first that
\beta\in Y(v,S)
if and only if
Bvx_{S}=Bvx_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}
.
It is clear that if we take the element
b\in B_{L}
for which
\supp(be_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}-e_{S})>\beta
, then
\supp(be_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}-e_{S})\in\Psi\setminus\Phi^{+}(v)
which means that
B_{v}e_{S}=B_{v}e_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}
and that’s the same as
Bvx_{S}=Bvx_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}
.
On the other hand by
3
in definition
7.1
we have
Bvx_{S}=Bvx_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}\Rightarrow B_{v}e_{S}=B_{v}e_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}\Rightarrow\beta\in Y(v,S)
.

It follows that, if
\beta\in Y(v,S)
then
Bvx_{S}
is the open orbit and

\dim Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}=\dim Bvx_{S}-1

By inductive hypothesis
\dim Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}=\#\Psi+\#Y(s_{\alpha}v,S)
.
We conclude by noting that
Y(s_{\alpha}v,S)=Y(v,S)\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}
.

If instead
\beta\notin Y(v,S)
, then
Bv\exp(e_{S}+te_{\beta})\neq Bvx_{S}
and it is the open orbit.
It follows

\dim Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}=\dim Bvx_{S}

as before
\dim Bs_{\alpha}vx_{S\setminus\left\{\beta\right\}}=\#\Psi+\#Y(s_{\alpha}v,S)
, but now
Y(s_{\alpha}v,S)=Y(v,S)
and we are done.
∎

Corollary 7.4
.

\dim B_{v}e_{S}=\#\left(\Psi\setminus\Phi^{+}(v)\right)+\#Y(v,S)

Proof.

It is sufficient to note that
\#\Phi^{+}(v)=l(v)
.
∎

Now consider the (good) parametrization of
[
4
]
in admissible pairs.
We have two different ways of computing the dimension, that is

\dim(Bvx_{S})=\#\Psi+L(\sigma_{v(S)})

and

\dim(Bvx_{S})=\#\Psi+\#Y(v,S)

This means that if the characteristic is different from
2
it must be

\#Y(v,S)=L(\sigma_{v(S)})

It follows that studying how
Y(v,S)
varies for similar orbits when the characteristic is (or isn’t)
2
should give us a more explicit formula.

We will study at first the case where the characteristic of the base field
\mathbb{K}
is not
2
.
Then we will be able to give another description of
Y(v,S)
that is more combinatoric

Lemma 7.5
.

If
\ch\mathbb{K}\neq 2
we have

Y(v,S)=\left\{\beta\in\Phi^{+}(v)\mid\beta\in S\text{ or there is }\alpha\in S\text{ such that }\beta-\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\right\}

Proof.

The containment
\supseteq
is clear whenever
\beta\in S
, while if there is
\alpha\in S
such that
\beta-\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
, then
\beta-\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{P}
, so
u_{\beta-\alpha}(t)\subseteq B_{L}
and there is
t\in\mathbb{K}
such that
\supp(u_{\beta-\alpha}(t).e_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}-e_{S})>\beta
.
Note that
u_{\beta-\alpha}(t)
fixes all
e_{\gamma}
with
\gamma\in S,\gamma\neq\alpha
because of orthogonality.

For the converse, suppose
\beta\in Y(v,S)
but
\beta\notin S
and for every
\alpha\in S
either
\beta-\alpha
is not a root or it is a negative root.
Then denote with
v_{\beta}\in W^{P}
the smallest element for which
\beta
is maximal, or, equivalently, the only element for which
\beta
is the maximum of
\Phi^{+}(v_{\beta})
and put
S_{\beta}=S\cap\Phi^{+}(v_{\beta})
.
By the fact that
\beta\in Y(v,S)
we have
b\in B_{L}
such that
\supp(be_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}-e_{S})>\beta
, but
B_{L}\subseteq B_{v_{\beta}}
so
B_{v_{\beta}}e_{S_{\beta}}=B_{v_{\beta}}e_{S_{\beta}\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}
and that’s a contradiction because
\beta
is orthogonal to all
\alpha\in S_{\beta}
.
∎

We saw that when the root system is of type
\bf{B}
, the set of orbit is small enough to make viable a case by case analysis.
Concordantly, it is possible to have a similar analysis for the dimension of the orbits.

Corollary 7.6

(Dimension formula for type
\bf{B}
)
.

If
\ch(\mathbb{K})=2
and the root system is of type
B
then

\dim(Bvx_{S})=\#\Psi+H(v,S)

where

H(v,S)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}L(\sigma_{v(S)})&\text{ if }S\text{ is orthogonal and }S\neq\left\{\alpha_{0}\right\}\\
L(\sigma_{v(s_{\alpha_{0}})})-\#\left\{\alpha_{0}<\alpha<\gamma\right\}&\text{ if }S=\left\{\alpha_{0},\gamma\right\}\\
L(\sigma_{v(s_{\alpha_{0}})})-\#\left\{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}(v)\mid\alpha_{0}<\alpha\right\}&\text{ if }S=\left\{\alpha_{0}\right\}\end{array}\right.

We also have a similar result for the orbits in type
\bf{C}
.

Theorem 7.7

(Dimension formula for type
\bf{C}
)
.

Fix
v\in W^{P}
and
S
full admissible for
v
.
Then:

\dim(Bvx_{S})=\#\Psi+L(\sigma_{v(X(S))})-\#S_{s}+\#Z(S)

Proof.

We can then apply theorem
7.3
to get

\dim(Bvx_{S})=\#\Psi+\#Y(v,S)

We want to give another description on
Y(v,S)
.
We claim that

Y(v,S)=\left\{\beta\in\Phi^{+}(v)\mid\beta\in S\text{ or }\beta\text{ is short and there is }\alpha\in X(S)\text{ such that }\beta-\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\right\}

The containment
\supseteq
is clear if
\beta\in S
, so suppose
\beta
short and
\alpha\in X(S)
such that
\gamma=\beta-\alpha
is a positive root.
Note that
u_{\beta-\alpha}(t)
fixes all
e_{\gamma}
with
\gamma\in X(S),\gamma\neq\alpha
because of orthogonality and if
\suc(\alpha)\in S
than
\supp(u_{\beta-\alpha}(t).e_{\suc(\alpha)}-e_{\suc(\alpha)})>\beta
.
Then there is
t\in\mathbb{K}
such that
\supp(u_{\beta-\alpha}(t).e_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}-e_{S})>\beta
.

On the other hand suppose
\beta\in Y(v,S)
, and suppose at first by contradiction that
\beta\notin S,\beta
long.
Then,
S
being full admissible
S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}
is admissible and its full admissible completion represent a different orbit, so
\beta\notin Y(v,S)
.

Suppose instead
\beta
short and for every
\alpha\in X(S)
either
\beta-\alpha
is not a root or it is a negative root.
Then for every
\alpha\in X(S)
either
(\alpha,\beta)=0
or
\beta<\alpha
.
So, consider
v_{\beta}\in W^{P}
the element such that the unique maximal root in
\Phi^{+}(v_{\beta})
is
\beta
.
By the fact that
\beta\in Y(v,S)
we have
b\in B_{L}
such that
\supp(be_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}-e_{S})>\beta
, but
b\in B_{v_{\beta}}
so
B_{v_{\beta}}e_{S}=B_{v_{\beta}}e_{S\cup\left\{\beta\right\}}
and that’s a contradiction because
\beta
is orthogonal to all
\alpha\in X(S)\cap\Phi^{+}(v_{\beta})
.

We now recall that, by lemma
7.5
,

L(\sigma_{v(X(S))})=\#\left\{\beta\in\Phi^{+}(v)\mid\beta\in S\text{ or there is }\alpha\in S\text{ such that }\beta-\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\right\}

It follows that in this case
\#Y(v,S)
is exactly
L(\sigma_{v(X(S))})
minus the number of long roots
\beta\notin S
such that there is
\alpha\in S
and
\beta-\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
.
This is exactly the number of short roots
\alpha
in
S
for which
\suc(\alpha)\notin S
or alternatively, the number of all short roots in
S
minus the number of roots in
Z(S)
.
∎
